STICK-FIGURE STORY STUDIO

AI image & video production script

6 story series · 22 parts · 1 minute 30 seconds per part · 12 shots per part

Children's Section

C1 The Foolish King of Andher Nagari (3 parts) · C2 Birbal the Clever (3 parts)

Adult Section

A1 The Cop and the Anthem (3 parts) · A2 The Gift of the Magi (3 parts)

Fascinating Section

F1 Kaspar Hauser: The Boy From Nowhere (4 parts) · F2 Casanova's Escape From the Leads (6 parts)

Visual style matched to the supplied reference video: round white-headed stick figures on detailed, warm-lit cartoon backgrounds, with greyscale pencil-sketch flashbacks and a reusable end card.

Companion document: ‘Stick-Figure Story Studio – ElevenLabs + Google Flow (under 60 s)’.

  

# 1\. How to use this script – step by step

1\. Pick your tools. You need (a) an image generator that accepts reference images, such as ChatGPT image generation, Google Gemini or Flow, Midjourney, Leonardo, Ideogram or Higgsfield; (b) an image-to-video animator, such as Kling, Runway, Hailuo, Higgsfield or Google Flow; (c) a voice tool, such as ElevenLabs or CapCut text-to-speech; and (d) an editor, such as CapCut.

2\. Set the format. Everything is vertical 9:16. Generate images at the tool's tallest 9:16 size and export the final video at 1080 × 1920, 30 fps.

3\. Lock the style once. Paste the MASTER STYLE (section 2) at the start of each chat-based image session. Every scene prompt in this script already carries its own Style line, so it also works in tools that do not remember context. If your tool has a negative-prompt box, paste the NEGATIVE PROMPT there.

4\. Make the end card first. Generate the Storyteller end card (section 2.6) once. You reuse the same image and clip as Shot 12 in every part.

5\. Make character sheets before any scenes. For each series, paste every Character Sheet prompt, generate 2–4 options and keep the best one. Save it as e.g. ‘C1\_GURU.png’. This is the single most important step for keeping characters identical across parts.

6\. Make location plates. Paste each Location prompt (empty background, no characters). Save the best one. Use it as a background or style reference for the shots in that place.

7\. Generate the shots in order. For each shot, paste the IMAGE PROMPT and attach the matching character sheet(s), plus the location plate if your tool allows several references. Generate 2–4 options, pick the one that best matches the narration line, and check it against the checklist in section 2.8. Name files e.g. ‘C1\_P1\_S03.png’.

8\. Animate each shot. Upload the image into your animator with its MOTION PROMPT (the Motion line plus the standard motion suffix in section 2.4). Generate a 5 s or 10 s clip and trim it to the shot's duration in the timing column.

9\. Record the voiceover. Paste the part's FULL NARRATION block into your voice tool, using the series' narrator direction. Aim for about 80–85 seconds of speech. If it runs long, speed it up to 1.05–1.1× rather than cutting lines.

10\. Edit. In CapCut, lay the clips in shot order against the voiceover using the time codes. Add bold auto-captions in the lower third, the TEXT overlays listed in each shot (titles, dates, names, signs), the sound effects, and music at about –20 dB under the voice. Finish with the end card and a ‘FOLLOW – Part X next’ button.

11\. Match the reference video's pace. The reference cuts roughly every 3 seconds. For any shot longer than 6 s, split it into two cuts: the full frame, then a 120–130 % punch-in on a face or detail. You can also regenerate the same image with a different expression.

12\. Export and post. Export at 1080 × 1920, 30 fps. Turn on the platform's AI-generated content label. Use the post caption given for each part, and post the parts in order (daily works well).

  

# 2\. Master style guide

## 2.1 Master style (set-up prompt)

MASTER STYLE (use this to set up the look, and paste it before your first prompt in any chat-based image tool):  
A polished 2D cartoon stick-figure animation style for vertical short-form video. CHARACTERS: every person has a large, perfectly round, pure-white head drawn with a thick, smooth black outline (no skin colour, no shading on the head except a faint grey under-shadow). Faces are simple but very expressive: big white eyes with solid black pupils, thick black eyebrows that carry the emotion, and a clear mouth shape (grin, scream, frown, gritted teeth). Bodies, arms and legs are thin, solid-black stick lines of even weight; hands are round black mittens with simple fingers when needed; feet are small black ovals. Hair, hats, beards and clothes sit on top of the stick body as simple flat-colour shapes with black outlines, so each character is recognisable from its silhouette. Use exaggerated cartoon emotion symbols when the moment calls for it: blue sweat drops, shake lines, motion puffs, anger steam, sparkles, question marks, a lightbulb. BACKGROUNDS: detailed, flat-colour, cel-shaded cartoon environments with clean dark outlines, soft gradients, believable furniture and props, and warm motivated light sources (lamps, candles, windows, moonlight, sunsets). Gentle vignette. COMPOSITION: vertical 9:16, characters large enough to read on a phone, main action in the middle two-thirds of the frame, clear space in the lower third for captions. NEVER: photorealism, 3D, anime, skin-coloured heads, text or watermarks.

## 2.2 Style line used inside every scene prompt

Style: 2D cartoon stick-figure animation still. Every character has a big, perfectly round, pure-white head with a thick, clean black outline and a simple, expressive cartoon face (large white eyes, solid black pupils, bold black eyebrows, clear mouth shape). Bodies, arms and legs are thin solid-black stick lines with round black hands and small oval black feet. Clothes, hair and hats are simple flat-colour shapes with black outlines. Detailed flat-colour, cel-shaded cartoon background with soft gradients and warm, motivated lighting. Vertical 9:16 frame. No text, no letters, no logo, no watermark.

## 2.3 Flashback / memory style

Used for memories and imagined scenes (marked FLASHBACK in the shot list), like the grey pencil-sketch segment in the reference video. {accent} is filled in per shot.

Style: the same 2D cartoon stick-figure look, rendered as a greyscale pencil-sketch flashback: soft graphite shading, off-white paper tone, slightly faded edges. Everything is grey except {accent}, which stays in full colour. Round white heads with thick black outlines, thin black stick limbs. Vertical 9:16 frame. No text, no letters, no logo, no watermark.

## 2.4 Negative prompt and motion suffix

NEGATIVE PROMPT: photorealistic, photo, 3D render, CGI, realistic skin, skin-coloured head, human anatomy, anime, manga, chibi, missing outline, extra limbs, extra fingers, extra heads, deformed face, crossed eyes, blurry, low resolution, text, letters, words, numbers, captions, speech-bubble text, logo, watermark, signature, frame border, split panels

ADD TO EVERY MOTION PROMPT: Smooth, simple 2D cartoon animation. Keep every face, outline, colour and proportion exactly as in the image. No morphing, no new characters, no text.

SHORT STYLE (for tools with strict prompt limits, e.g. Midjourney; add --ar 9:16): 2D cartoon stick-figure style, round pure-white heads with thick black outlines, expressive faces, thin black stick limbs, detailed flat cel-shaded background, warm lighting, 9:16, no text

## 2.5 Technical specification

Frame: 9:16 vertical, 1080 × 1920 final, 30 fps.

Runtime: 90 seconds per part = 11 story shots (85 s, timed to the narration) + 1 end card (5 s).

Narration: about 150–200 words per part, roughly 2–2.4 words per second, which leaves room for sound-effect beats.

Captions: large bold white text with a black outline, 2–4 words per line, in the lower third (the prompts keep that area clear).

Text inside images: AI generators garble lettering, so every prompt says ‘no text’. Add all titles, dates, names and signs in the editor (listed as TEXT in each shot).

Audio: voice at 0 dB peak, music –18 to –22 dB, sound effects –10 dB. Use royalty-free music only (e.g. CapCut library, YouTube Audio Library).

## 2.6 End card – the Storyteller (generate once, reuse as Shot 12)

CHARACTER SHEET – HOST: the Storyteller: a friendly stick-figure host with a big round pure-white head, a warm wide smile, small round black reading glasses, a short red knitted scarf and a thin black stick body, holding an open storybook.

END-CARD IMAGE PROMPT: Medium-wide shot. The Storyteller stands on the left third of the frame in a cosy story room: wooden bookshelves full of colourful books, a warm brass desk lamp, framed pictures, a soft round rug and a comfortable armchair. He smiles at the viewer, waving with one hand and pointing to the empty right side of the frame with the other. Keep the right half of the frame clean and uncluttered for on-screen buttons that will be added in editing. Character – the Storyteller: a friendly stick-figure host with a big round pure-white head, a warm wide smile, small round black reading glasses, a short red knitted scarf and a thin black stick body, holding an open storybook. Style: 2D cartoon stick-figure animation still. Every character has a big, perfectly round, pure-white head with a thick, clean black outline and a simple, expressive cartoon face (large white eyes, solid black pupils, bold black eyebrows, clear mouth shape). Bodies, arms and legs are thin solid-black stick lines with round black hands and small oval black feet. Clothes, hair and hats are simple flat-colour shapes with black outlines. Detailed flat-colour, cel-shaded cartoon background with soft gradients and warm, motivated lighting. Vertical 9:16 frame. No text, no letters, no logo, no watermark.

END-CARD MOTION PROMPT: The Storyteller waves, then points to the right with a big smile and a small bounce; the lamp light flickers softly; slow gentle push-in. Smooth, simple 2D cartoon animation. Keep every face, outline, colour and proportion exactly as in the image. No morphing, no new characters, no text.

Overlay in the editor: a red ‘FOLLOW’ button, a heart, a share arrow and ‘PART X NEXT’ on the empty right half. This host is original to this channel; do not copy another creator's characters or watermark.

## 2.7 Character-sheet prompt template

Character reference sheet of \[CHARACTER DESCRIPTION\]. Show the same character four times in a row: front view, three-quarter view, side view and back view, standing in a neutral pose, plus a row of four head close-ups showing happy, angry, scared and surprised expressions. Plain light-grey background, even lighting, no props unless described. Style: 2D cartoon stick-figure animation still. Every character has a big, perfectly round, pure-white head with a thick, clean black outline and a simple, expressive cartoon face (large white eyes, solid black pupils, bold black eyebrows, clear mouth shape). Bodies, arms and legs are thin solid-black stick lines with round black hands and small oval black feet. Clothes, hair and hats are simple flat-colour shapes with black outlines. Detailed flat-colour, cel-shaded cartoon background with soft gradients and warm, motivated lighting. Vertical 9:16 frame. No text, no letters, no logo, no watermark.

Each series section below has the template already filled in for every character.

## 2.8 Quality checklist (run on every image)

Head is a clean round white circle with a thick black outline: no skin colour, no nose, no ears.

Limbs are thin black sticks; no extra fingers, arms or legs.

Costume, hair and props match the character sheet exactly (crown crooked, bowler hat battered, and so on).

Expression matches the narration line; exaggerate it if in doubt.

Background matches the location plate and era; no modern objects in historical stories.

No garbled text, logos or watermarks; lower third is clear for captions.

Violence is implied, never shown: no blood, wounds or weapons in close-up.

### Material supplied but not adapted

Shirley Jackson, ‘The Lottery’ (1948): still in copyright. Changing it slightly would still make it an adaptation, so it is left out.

Sudha Murty, ‘Grandma's Bag of Stories’ (2012): still in copyright. The children's section instead retells much older traditional folk tales from scratch. Murty's own ‘Kingdom of Fools’ is a different story from the traditional Andher Nagari tale used here.

Stacy Aumonier, ‘Extremely Entertaining Short Stories’ sample: the stories (1914–1928) are public domain, but the sample PDF holds only the first two or three pages of each, so none could be adapted in full. Supply the complete text of e.g. ‘Miss Bracegirdle Does Her Duty’ to add it.

O. Henry PDF: the file named ‘private-project-details-100-images’ is O. Henry's ‘100 Selected Stories’ (public domain). More strong candidates for future series: ‘The Last Leaf’, ‘The Skylight Room’, ‘A Municipal Report’.

Andrew Lang, ‘The True Story Book’ (1893): more candidates: ‘The Story of Grace Darling’ (good for children), ‘Captain Snelgrave and the Pirates’, ‘The Worthy Enterprise of John Foxe’.

This is a practical summary, not legal advice. Platform rules on AI-generated content change; check the current rules before posting.

  

CHILDREN'S SECTION

Warm, funny folk tales for young viewers (about 6 and up).

  

# C1 · The Foolish King of Andher Nagari

Logline: A greedy young disciple stays in a city where everything costs one coin – until the silly king needs a fat neck for the Royal Stocks.

Source: Traditional North Indian folk tale (‘Andher Nagari, Chaupat Raja’ – the City of Darkness and its Muddled King). Retold from scratch for this series; not based on any modern published version.

Rights: Traditional folklore – public domain. This retelling is original.

Parts: 3 × 1 minute 30 seconds

Narrator voice: Warm, cheerful storyteller (a kind grandparent). Medium pace. Give the King a sleepy, pompous voice and Govardhan a squeaky, greedy one.

Music: Light, playful Indian-flavoured music (sitar, tabla, flute) at low volume; comic stings on gags.

## C1 · Part 1 of 3 – The City Where Everything Cost One Coin

Runtime: 1:30 · 12 shots · narration 187 words

Post caption: A city where EVERYTHING costs one coin… what could go wrong? ð¬ \#storytime \#folktales \#stickman

### Full narration (paste into your voice tool)

Long ago, an old guru and his young disciple, Govardhan, walked into a strange city. The city was called Andher Nagari, and it was ruled by a very muddled king. In the market, Govardhan heard a seller shouting: 'Everything costs one coin\! Vegetables, one coin\! Sweets, one coin\!' Govardhan's eyes went huge. For one single coin, he bought a whole basket of sweet, sticky laddoos. But the guru frowned. 'Where gold and grass cost the same, there is no sense. Let us leave.' 'Leave? Never\!' said Govardhan. 'I'm staying right here, where the sweets are cheap\!' The guru sighed. 'Very well. But if you ever find trouble here, think of me and call my name.' So Govardhan stayed. Breakfast, sweets. Lunch, sweets. Dinner, more sweets. Day after day after day. Months passed. His belly grew round, his cheeks grew round, and even his neck grew round and thick. Meanwhile, the muddled king made muddled laws, and nobody dared to argue with him. Then one dark night, in that silly city, a wall came crashing down… and trouble began. Follow for Part 2\!

## C1 · Part 2 of 3 – The Silliest Trial Ever

Runtime: 1:30 · 12 shots · narration 197 words

Post caption: A wall fell on a burglar… so the king put the WALL on trial ð \#storytime \#folktales

### Full narration (paste into your voice tool)

Previously: Govardhan stayed in the foolish city and grew round on cheap sweets. Then a wall fell. A burglar had been climbing into a rich merchant's house when the wall fell on him. Crash\! Ouch\! His brother marched to the palace. 'Your Majesty\! A wall attacked my brother\! I demand justice\!' The king never asked why a burglar was climbing walls. He shouted, 'Bring me the wall's owner\!' The merchant trembled. 'Not my fault, Your Majesty\! The mason built the wall badly\!' The mason gulped. 'Not me\! The water-carrier poured too much water into my cement\!' 'Not me\!' cried the water-carrier. 'The king's own elephant trumpeted so loudly, I jumped and spilled it\!' Everyone gasped. The king's elephant? The king thought hard… then pointed at the elephant's skinny keeper. 'Guilty\!' shouted the king. 'Lock him in the Royal Stocks for one hundred days\!' But the keeper was so skinny that his neck slipped straight out of the hole. Again, and again\! 'Fool\!' roared the king. 'Find me someone with a big, fat neck that fits\!' And the guards looked… at Govardhan. Follow for Part 3\!

## C1 · Part 3 of 3 – The Guru's Clever Trick

Runtime: 1:30 · 12 shots · narration 202 words

Post caption: How do you get a greedy king to lock HIMSELF up? ð¤ \#storytime \#folktales \#moral

### Full narration (paste into your voice tool)

Previously: the king needed a fat neck for the stocks, and the guards grabbed Govardhan\! Govardhan wailed. 'Oh, why didn't I listen to my guru?' Then he remembered: call his name\! Just then, through the crowd, came the old guru, tapping his staff. He whispered a secret in Govardhan's ear. Then the guru shouted, 'Wait\! Put ME in the stocks\!' 'No\!' cried Govardhan. 'It must be me\!' The king was puzzled. 'Why on earth would anyone WANT to go into the stocks?' The guru bowed. 'Your Majesty, the stars say whoever sits in these stocks at this very hour will rule the whole world\!' The king's eyes sparkled. 'Rule the whole world? Out of the way\! Nobody gets that but me\!' 'Wait for me\!' cried the minister, and the two of them squeezed into the stocks together. Click\! The lock snapped shut, and the key went plop, straight into the well\! The whole city laughed and laughed. At last they saw who the real fools were, and chose wise new leaders. And Govardhan? He walked home with his guru, and never again chose cheap sweets over good sense. Follow for more stories like this\!

# C2 · Birbal the Clever

Logline: Emperor Akbar's sharpest minister solves a theft, a riddle about a beard, an impossible chalk line and an unfair reward – using nothing but his brain.

Source: Traditional Akbar–Birbal folk tales from Mughal India (16th century court of Emperor Akbar). Three classic tales, retold from scratch for this series.

Rights: Traditional folklore – public domain. This retelling is original.

Parts: 3 × 1 minute 30 seconds

Narrator voice: Warm, lively storyteller. Akbar sounds grand and amused; Birbal calm, clever and a little cheeky.

Music: Playful Mughal-court flavour (santoor, tabla, light strings); a bright ‘ding’ whenever Birbal solves the puzzle.

## C2 · Part 1 of 3 – The Magic Sticks

Runtime: 1:30 · 12 shots · narration 162 words

Post caption: Five servants. One thief. One very clever trick ðª \#storytime \#birbal \#kids

### Full narration (paste into your voice tool)

Long ago, Emperor Akbar's favourite ruby ring vanished from his room. Only five servants had been inside that room. One of them was a thief. But which one? The emperor called for his cleverest minister, Birbal. Birbal smiled. 'Leave it to me, Your Majesty.' Birbal gave each servant a wooden stick. Every stick was exactly the same length. 'These are magic sticks,' he said. 'By morning, the thief's stick will grow one finger longer\!' That night, everyone slept peacefully… everyone except one. 'If my stick grows, they'll catch me\!' he whispered. So he cut one finger off the end. Next morning, the servants lined up, and Birbal compared the sticks. Four sticks were exactly the same. But one stick was now one finger shorter\! 'The sticks were never magic,' laughed Birbal. 'Only a guilty man would cut his.' Emperor Akbar laughed out loud. 'Birbal, your brain is sharper than any sword\!' Follow for Part 2\!

## C2 · Part 2 of 3 – The Beard and the Line

Runtime: 1:30 · 12 shots · narration 177 words

Post caption: Who dared to pull the EMPEROR'S beard? ð³ \#storytime \#birbal \#riddles

### Full narration (paste into your voice tool)

One morning, Emperor Akbar arrived at court with a strange question. 'Someone pulled my beard this morning,' he said. 'What punishment does he deserve?' 'Throw him in prison\!' shouted one courtier. 'Banish him to the desert\!' cried another. But Birbal just smiled. 'Your Majesty, give him sweets and a big hug.' 'Sweets?' the courtiers cried. Birbal said, 'Only one person would dare pull the emperor's beard: his little grandson\!' Akbar roared with laughter. 'Right again, Birbal\!' And the jealous courtiers turned as red as tomatoes. Another day, Akbar drew a chalk line on the floor. 'Make this line shorter,' he said, 'without touching it.' The courtiers scratched their heads. Rub it out? Not allowed\! Cut it? Impossible\! Birbal picked up the chalk and quietly drew a much longer line, right beside it. 'There,' he said. 'Your line is untouched… but now it is the shorter one.' Akbar clapped. 'Brilliant\! To win, you don't need to shrink others. Just grow yourself\!' Follow for Part 3\!

## C2 · Part 3 of 3 – Birbal's Khichdi

Runtime: 1:30 · 12 shots · narration 196 words

Post caption: Can a lamp a mile away keep you warm? Birbal has an answer ð² \#storytime \#birbal

### Full narration (paste into your voice tool)

One freezing winter, Emperor Akbar made a strange offer. 'Whoever stands all night in the icy palace lake,' he announced, 'will win one thousand gold coins\!' Nobody dared, until a poor washerman stepped forward. His family was hungry and needed that gold. All night he stood in the freezing water, teeth chattering, shivering. But he did not come out. In the morning Akbar asked, 'How did you survive?' 'I looked at a lamp on the palace wall, far away.' 'A lamp?' said Akbar. 'Then you were warmed by its heat. No gold for you\!' The poor man went home with nothing. That day, Birbal did not come to court. Akbar went looking, and found Birbal outside, staring up at a pot hanging high on a tall pole. 'I'm cooking khichdi,' said Birbal. 'It will be ready soon.' 'From that far away?' laughed Akbar. Birbal smiled. 'If a far-off lamp can warm a man in a frozen lake, surely this fire can cook my rice.' Akbar understood at once. The washerman got his gold, and a warm new shawl too. Follow for more stories like this\!

# A1 · The Cop and the Anthem

Logline: A New York tramp tries everything to get arrested so he can spend the winter warm in jail – and fails, until the one moment he decides to change his life.

Source: O. Henry, ‘The Cop and the Anthem’ (1904), from the supplied ‘100 Selected Stories’ PDF. Adapted and condensed in original wording.

Rights: Public domain (O. Henry died in 1910).

Parts: 3 × 1 minute 30 seconds

Narrator voice: Dry, witty narrator with comic timing. Light old-New-York accents for the characters. Medium-fast pace.

Music: Jaunty ragtime piano under the attempts; it stops dead for the church scene, which uses a soft organ hymn; a sting on the final line.

## A1 · Part 1 of 3 – Three Months of Free Winter

Runtime: 1:30 · 12 shots · narration 183 words

Post caption: This man is TRYING to get arrested… and failing ð Part 1 \#storytime \#ohenry

### Full narration (paste into your voice tool)

In Madison Square, New York, a dead leaf dropped into Soapy's lap. Winter was coming. Rich New Yorkers spent winter in sunny Florida. Soapy had humbler plans: three months on 'the Island'. The Island was the city prison. Warm beds, hot meals, and nobody asking questions. Perfect\! The plan was simple: get arrested. And the nicest way? A fancy dinner he couldn't pay for. Roast duck, he thought. Wine, cheese, coffee and a cigar. Then: 'Officer, I'm broke\!' But the moment he stepped inside, the head waiter spotted his frayed trousers and broken shoes. Two strong hands spun him round and put him straight back on the pavement. Silently. Quickly. Plan two. On Sixth Avenue, Soapy picked up a cobblestone and hurled it through a shop window. Crash\! A policeman came running. Soapy waited, hands in his pockets, smiling. 'Maybe it was me, officer?' But window-smashers don't wait around to chat. The policeman ran off after a man chasing a streetcar. Soapy sighed. Two tries, two failures. Surely getting arrested couldn't be this hard… could it? Follow for Part 2\!

### Shot list

## A1 · Part 2 of 3 – Doomed to Freedom

Runtime: 1:30 · 12 shots · narration 184 words

Post caption: He ate a huge meal and said 'call a cop'. They didn't. ð­ Part 2 \#storytime \#ohenry

### Full narration (paste into your voice tool)

Previously: Soapy wanted three warm months in prison. But the police kept ignoring him. So he walked into a cheap diner, where nobody checked his shoes, and ordered everything. Then he smiled at the waiter. 'I haven't got a penny. Go on, call a cop.' No cop. Instead, two waiters threw him out, and he landed on his ear on the cold pavement. A policeman nearby just laughed and strolled away. Soapy got up slowly, joint by joint. Next, he spotted a young lady at a shop window, and a big policeman watching close by. Soapy put on his worst manners: winking, coughing, tipping his hat, pestering her like a rude flirt. Surely she'd call the officer. Instead, she grabbed his sleeve and beamed. 'Sure\! If you'll buy me a beer\!' Soapy walked right past the cop with her on his arm, then broke free and ran\! Outside a glittering theatre, he danced, howled and yelled nonsense. Disorderly conduct, for sure\! The policeman just shrugged. 'College boys celebrating. Leave them be.' Was Soapy cursed to stay free? Follow for Part 3\!

## A1 · Part 3 of 3 – The Anthem

Runtime: 1:30 · 12 shots · narration 198 words

Post caption: He finally decided to change his life. Then THIS happened. ð¤¯ Final part \#storytime \#ohenry

### Full narration (paste into your voice tool)

Previously: Soapy tried everything to get arrested. Nothing worked. In a cigar shop, he saw a gentleman's silk umbrella by the door. He picked it up and strolled off. 'That's my umbrella\!' 'Then call a policeman,' said Soapy. 'There's one right there.' But the gentleman went pale. 'Er… I picked it up in a restaurant this morning. If it's yours, I'm terribly sorry\!' Furious, Soapy hurled the umbrella into a hole in the road and headed back towards his park bench. On a quiet corner stood an old church. Inside, the organist was practising a Sunday anthem. Soapy knew that tune. He'd heard it long ago, when he had a mother, and friends, and dreams. Suddenly he saw how low he had fallen. Tomorrow, he decided, he'd find work and be a man again. A fur dealer had once offered him a job driving. He'd go there first thing tomorrow. He would be somebody\! Then a hand landed on his arm. A policeman. 'What are you doing here?' 'Nothing.' 'Then come along.' Next morning, the magistrate banged his gavel. 'Three months on the Island.' Follow for more stories like this\!

## A2 · Part 1 of 3 – One Dollar and Eighty-Seven Cents

Runtime: 1:30 · 12 shots · narration 184 words

Post caption: $1.87 to buy the love of her life a Christmas gift… ð Part 1 \#storytime \#christmas

### Full narration (paste into your voice tool)

One dollar and eighty-seven cents. That was all Della had. And tomorrow was Christmas. She counted it three times. Months of saving, penny by penny, and still it was not enough. So Della did the only sensible thing. She flopped onto the shabby little couch and cried. Their flat cost eight dollars a week. Worn carpet, a broken doorbell, and a window onto a grey backyard. But they owned two treasures. The first was Jim's gold watch, his father's and grandfather's before him. The second was Della's hair. Long, shining, rippling brown hair that fell below her knees. It was so glorious that a queen living across the way would have been jealous of it. Della stared at her reflection. Her eyes shone. Then her face went pale. She had an idea. Quickly, she pinned her hair up. A tear or two splashed onto the worn red carpet. On went her old brown jacket. On went her old brown hat. And out she ran, down the stairs, into the cold street, to a shop with a very particular sign. Follow for Part 2\!

## A2 · Part 2 of 3 – Twenty Dollars

Runtime: 1:30 · 12 shots · narration 196 words

Post caption: She sold the one thing she loved most… ð Part 2 \#storytime \#christmas

### Full narration (paste into your voice tool)

Previously: with only one dollar eighty-seven, Della ran out into the snow with a secret plan. The sign read: Madame Sofronie, Hair Goods of All Kinds. Della climbed the stairs, out of breath. 'Will you buy my hair?' Madame looked her up and down. 'Take your hat off. Let's see it.' Down came the brown waterfall. Madame lifted it with an expert hand. 'Twenty dollars.' 'Give it to me quick,' said Della. Snip, snip, snip… and it was done. For the next two hours she searched every shop in town for Jim's present. At last she found it: a simple platinum watch chain. Plain, quiet and fine, just like Jim. It cost twenty-one dollars. She hurried home with eighty-seven cents and a heart full of joy. Then she looked in the mirror. Short, tight curls all over her head. 'Oh, what will Jim say?' Seven o'clock. The coffee was ready. She heard his steps on the stairs. 'Please, let him still think I'm pretty.' The door opened. Jim stepped in… and stopped. He stared at her with a look she could not read. Follow for Part 3\!

## A2 · Part 3 of 3 – The Wisest Gifts

Runtime: 1:30 · 12 shots · narration 202 words

Post caption: The most beautiful twist in any Christmas story ð Final part \#storytime \#christmas

### Full narration (paste into your voice tool)

Previously: Della sold her beautiful hair to buy Jim a Christmas present. Now he just stared. 'Jim, don't look at me like that\! I sold my hair to buy you a present. It'll grow back\!' Jim seemed to wake from a trance. He hugged her tight, then took a small package from his coat. 'Nothing could make me love you less,' he said. 'But open that, and you'll see why I froze.' Della tore it open and screamed with joy… then burst into tears. The jewelled combs she had dreamed of for months. And now she had no long hair to wear them in. She smiled through her tears. 'My hair grows fast, Jim\! Now, your present. Give me your watch\!' Jim sank onto the couch, put his hands behind his head, and smiled a slow, sad smile. 'Dell, let's put our presents away for a while. I sold my watch to buy your combs.' The Magi, the wise men, brought the very first Christmas gifts. These two gave up their greatest treasures. Foolish, some would say. But of all who give gifts, these two were the wisest. They gave love. Follow for more stories like this\!

## F1 · Part 1 of 4 – The Boy Who Came From Nowhere

Runtime: 1:30 · 12 shots · narration 191 words

Post caption: 1828: a boy appears from nowhere holding a mysterious letter ð Part 1 \#truestory \#mystery \#history

### Full narration (paste into your voice tool)

Nuremberg, Germany. Whit Monday, 26 May 1828. A holiday. The streets were almost empty. A cobbler stepped outside for a walk, and saw something strange leaning against a wall. A boy of about sixteen, swaying and moaning, unable to speak. In his hand: a letter. The letter was addressed to a cavalry captain. So the cobbler led the boy to the captain's house. Offered meat and beer, he refused them. He would eat only bread and drink only water. The letter said a poor labourer had kept him locked away since 1812, letting him see and know nothing. It ended coldly: make him a soldier, or hang him, or put him up the chimney. As you please. Asked his name, the boy only repeated a few phrases, like a parrot. 'I want to be a soldier.' 'Horse.' The police locked him in a prison tower, suspecting a trick. Then a jailer gave him paper and a pencil. He grabbed them and wrote, slowly and carefully, two words: Kaspar Hauser. He had a name. But who was he? And where had he been for sixteen years? Follow for Part 2\!

## F1 · Part 2 of 4 – The Boy From the Dark

Runtime: 1:30 · 12 shots · narration 204 words

Post caption: He grew up in a dark cell and never saw a face ð¶️ Part 2 \#truestory \#mystery

### Full narration (paste into your voice tool)

Previously: a strange boy appeared in Nuremberg, unable to speak, carrying a mysterious letter. Crowds came to stare. He had a nearly grown body, but the mind of a toddler. He reached for a candle flame and cried when it burned him. He looked behind a mirror for the other boy. His favourite things in the world were wooden horses. Visitors brought him dozens. Slowly he learned to talk, and told a shocking story. He had lived his whole life in a tiny, dark cell. He never saw daylight. He never saw the face of the man who brought his bread and water. One day the man taught him to write his name, then to stand, then carried him out into the world. Was it true? Doctors found the soles of his feet as soft as a baby's. He could even see in the dark. The city adopted him as 'the Child of Nuremberg', and kind Professor Daumer took him into his home. Kaspar blossomed. He wrote, he drew, and he grew cress in the garden, spelling out his own name. By October 1829 he was writing his life story. Then one day, he was found bleeding in the cellar. Follow for Part 3\!

## F1 · Part 3 of 4 – The Masked Man

Runtime: 1:30 · 12 shots · narration 180 words

Post caption: Someone wanted the boy from the dark cell SILENCED ð­ Part 3 \#truestory \#unsolved

### Full narration (paste into your voice tool)

Previously: Kaspar, the boy from the dark cell, was found wounded in a cellar. Shaking, he said a man with a black face had struck him on the head, then vanished. Someone had tried to kill him. Was it the man from the dark cell, afraid of what Kaspar might reveal? From then on, two policemen guarded him everywhere he went. He became famous, one of the sights of Nuremberg. Important visitors came just to meet him. In 1831 a rich English lord, Lord Stanhope, arrived. He showered Kaspar with gifts and promised to take him to England. Kaspar moved to the town of Ansbach. But England never came. The promises faded away. His new teachers expected a genius. When he struggled, they called him lazy, even a liar. He was given a dull job copying papers in a government office. He grew lonely and sad. Then, on 14 December 1833, a stranger stopped him in the street. 'Come to the court garden,' he whispered, 'and I will tell you who your parents were.' Follow for Part 4\!

## F1 · Part 4 of 4 – Murder in the Snow

Runtime: 1:30 · 12 shots · narration 196 words

Post caption: Prince or impostor? The murder that was never solved ❄️ Final part \#truestory \#unsolved

### Full narration (paste into your voice tool)

Previously: a stranger promised to tell Kaspar the secret of his birth. That afternoon Kaspar went to the court garden. The stranger led him to a stone monument among the trees. The stranger handed him a small purse. Kaspar fumbled, and it dropped into the snow. As he bent down, he felt a sharp blow in his side. When he looked up, the man was gone. Kaspar staggered home to his tutor, gasping, 'Man… garden… stabbed me… purse\!' In the snow they found the purse. Inside was a note, written backwards, signed with three letters: M. L. O. The wound was deep. Three days later, on 17 December 1833, Kaspar Hauser died. He was twenty-one. A huge reward was offered for the killer. No one ever claimed it. The stranger was never found. Some said Kaspar wounded himself for attention. Doctors said the blow was far too deep and strong. Others believed he was a stolen prince of Baden, hidden away so that someone else could take the throne. His gravestone calls him the riddle of his time. Prince or impostor? Nearly two centuries later, no one knows. Follow for more stories like this\!

# F2 · Casanova's Escape From the Leads

Logline: Venice, 1755. Locked under the burning lead roof of the Doge's Palace, Casanova spends fifteen months plotting one of history's most daring prison escapes.

Source: ‘Casanova's Escape’ in Andrew Lang (ed.), The True Story Book (1893), from the supplied PDF, drawn from Casanova's own account. Adapted in original wording.

Rights: Public domain (published 1893). Historical facts are free to use.

Parts: 6 × 1 minute 30 seconds

Narrator voice: Confident, adventurous documentary voice with a hint of mischief. Build tension in the roof scenes.

## F2 · Part 1 of 6 – Prisoner Under the Lead Roof

Runtime: 1:30 · 12 shots · narration 187 words

Post caption: Locked under a burning lead roof… then he felt a cold hand in the dark ð± Part 1 \#truestory \#history

### Full narration (paste into your voice tool)

Venice, July 1755. Giacomo Casanova, charming, clever and far too daring, was arrested at dawn. His crime? Owning books of magic. The Inquisitors locked him in the dreaded prison called the Leads. The Leads were cells right under the palace roof, covered in heavy sheets of lead that baked in the sun. His cell was so low he could not stand up straight. There was no bed. The heat was unbearable. Hours passed. Alone and furious, he stamped, shouted and raged like a madman. Exhausted, he lay down on the hard floor and fell asleep. At midnight he woke and reached for his handkerchief… and touched a hand. An ice-cold hand\! His hair stood on end. A dead body? Had they strangled someone and left him there as a warning? He grabbed the hand to pull the body closer… and it moved. It was his own left hand\! It had gone completely numb on the cold floor. A comic moment, but it frightened him. If his mind could play such tricks, he might go mad in here. He had to get out. Follow for Part 2\!

## F2 · Part 2 of 6 – The Secret Spike

Runtime: 1:30 · 12 shots · narration 203 words

Post caption: He turned rubbish into an escape tool… under the guards' noses ð️ Part 2 \#truestory \#history

### Full narration (paste into your voice tool)

Previously: Casanova was locked in the Leads, the prison under the roof of the Doge's Palace. Months passed. Then he was allowed a short daily walk in the dusty attic corridor outside his cell. Among the junk he spotted a piece of black marble, and a long iron bolt. He hid them both. For fifteen days he rubbed the bolt against the marble, until his arm ached and his shoulder burned. At last he had a sharp iron spike. He hid it deep in the straw of his armchair. His plan: dig a hole through the floor into the room below, then slip out when its door opened. But the guards swept his cell every morning. So he faked a terrible cough. 'The dust is killing me\!' He even pricked his finger and showed blood on his handkerchief. The doctor ordered: no more sweeping\! Next, a lamp for the long dark nights: oil from his salads, a wick from threads in his quilt. Lamp lit, he dragged his bed aside. Under it lay the floor. And the spike was in his hand. The digging was about to begin. But it would take months… if he wasn't caught first. Follow for Part 3\!

## F2 · Part 3 of 6 – The Hole in the Floor

Runtime: 1:30 · 12 shots · narration 182 words

Post caption: Two days before his escape… the worst 'good news' ever ð¬ Part 3 \#truestory \#history

### Full narration (paste into your voice tool)

Previously: Casanova made a secret spike and a lamp, and began to dig. Night after night he dug under his bed, hiding the wood chips in the corridor rubbish. One plank. Then a second plank. Then a third\! Three weeks of work. Underneath was hard marble. His spike couldn't break it. But he remembered an old trick: vinegar. The vinegar softened the cement, and the marble came loose. Only one last plank remained. Finally he could peep through a tiny hole into the room below. He chose his night: 27 August. But on 25 August, at noon, the bolts rattled. Laurent burst in, grinning. 'Good news, sir\!' 'You're moving to a lovely new cell, with windows and a view of Venice\!' Casanova felt sick. Hours later, Laurent stormed in, purple with rage. He'd found the hole. 'Where are your tools?' Casanova shrugged. 'Tools? Only you visit me, Laurent. So you must have given them to me.' Scared, Laurent hushed it up. Now guards tapped the walls and floor every day. But never the ceiling… Follow for Part 4\!

## F2 · Part 4 of 6 – Macaroni and a Monk

Runtime: 1:30 · 12 shots · narration 187 words

Post caption: He smuggled an iron spike… under a plate of macaroni ð Part 4 \#truestory \#history

### Full narration (paste into your voice tool)

Previously: Casanova's tunnel was discovered. But his secret spike was still hidden. Prisoners were allowed to swap books. Inside one, Casanova found a hidden note from another prisoner. With no pen or ink, he wrote back using his fingernail dipped in mulberry juice. His new pen-friend was Father Balbi, a monk locked in a nearby cell. Together, they made a plan. Balbi would dig through his own ceiling, cross the attic, and break into Casanova's cell from above. But first Balbi needed the spike. Casanova hid it inside a huge Bible, but it stuck out at both ends. His genius solution? A giant dish of hot, buttery macaroni, balanced right on top of the Bible. 'A gift for the monk,' he told Laurent. 'Don't spill a single drop\!' Laurent carried it slowly, eyes fixed on the butter, never noticing the spike right under his nose. Balbi got to work. He covered his ceiling with holy pictures, and hid the hole behind the biggest one. Then one day, Casanova's door opened, and the jailer pushed in a new cellmate. A spy. Follow for Part 5\!

## F2 · Part 5 of 6 – The Angel in the Ceiling

Runtime: 1:30 · 12 shots · narration 194 words

Post caption: He convinced a spy that an ANGEL was coming to free them ð¼ Part 5 \#truestory \#history

### Full narration (paste into your voice tool)

Previously: Father Balbi was digging towards Casanova's cell. Then a spy moved in. Casanova tested him. He gave the spy two harmless letters to deliver, after making him swear an oath. Within two days, the spy betrayed him, just as Casanova expected. So Casanova put on a show. He told the spy the Virgin Mary had appeared to him in a vision. 'You are forgiven,' he said, 'and an angel is coming. He will break through our roof and set us free.' 'The angel starts work at three o'clock.' And at three o'clock exactly… scrape, scrape, scrape. The terrified spy believed every word. Of course, the 'angel' was Father Balbi, digging away. On the night of 31 October, the angel arrived, feet first through the ceiling. He didn't look much like an angel. Together they broke through the attic and pushed up a great sheet of lead. Above them: the open sky. But the moon was too bright. They'd be seen. So they waited, and Casanova wrote a polite letter to the Inquisitors. The spy begged to stay behind. Then the moon set, and the escape began. Follow for Part 6\!

## F2 · Part 6 of 6 – Over the Rooftops of Venice

Runtime: 1:30 · 12 shots · narration 190 words

Post caption: Hanging by his fingertips over the canal… the great escape ð️ Final part \#truestory \#history

### Full narration (paste into your voice tool)

Previously: Casanova and Father Balbi broke through the prison roof. Now, the rooftops. They crawled across the steep, slippery lead roof, high above the canal, using the spike to grip. Balbi lost his hat. It rolled down the roof and splashed into the canal. 'A bad omen\!' he wailed. At midnight a church bell rang. It was the first of November, All Saints' Day. Casanova felt lucky. He wrenched the iron grating off a skylight and lowered Balbi inside on the bedsheet rope. Then he found a workman's ladder. Forcing it in, he slipped, and dangled over the canal by his fingertips\! With a superhuman heave, he pulled himself up and climbed down the ladder into the palace. Exhausted, he slept. At dawn they smashed through doors and archives, tearing a hole in one door. Casanova changed into his fine clothes and feathered hat, then poked his head out of a window. People saw him and fetched the keeper, thinking a visitor was locked in. The door opened… Down the grand staircase, into a gondola, and away across the lagoon. Casanova was free\! Follow for more stories like this\!
